Database Management Systems
Introduction to NoSQL Systems

Prof. Shilpa S
Department of Computer Science and Engineering

Database Management Systems
NoSQL Databases
● NoSQL databases ("not only SQL") are non-tabular databases and store data

differently than relational tables.
● NoSQL databases come in a variety of types based on their data model. The

main types are document, key-value, wide-column, and graph.
● NoSQL databases emerged as the cost of storage dramatically decreased.
●

As storage costs rapidly decreased, the amount of data that applications
needed to store and query increased. This data came in all shapes and sizes

— structured, semi-structured, and polymorphic — and defining the schema
in advance became nearly impossible.

Database Management Systems
NoSQL Databases
● NoSQL databases allow developers to store huge amounts of unstructured

data, giving them a lot of flexibility.
● Most NOSQL systems are distributed databases or distributed storage

systems, focusing on semi-structured data storage, high performance,
availability, data replication, and scalability instead of emphasizing
immediate data consistency, powerful query languages, and structured
data storage.

Database Management Systems
Emergence of NoSQL systems
•
•

•
•

•

Many companies and organizations are faced with applications that store vast
amounts of data.
Consider a free email application, such as Google Mail or Yahoo Mail —this
application can have millions of users, and each user can have thousands of email
messages.
There is a need for a storage system that can manage all these emails
A structured relational SQL system may not be appropriate because:
(1) SQL systems offer too many services (powerful query language,
concurrency control, etc.), which this application may not need
(2) A structured data model, such as the traditional relational model, may be
too restrictive.
Hence, a NOSQL system with a dynamic schema and not many restrictions is
required.
4

Database Management Systems
Emergence of NoSQL systems
❖ Google developed a NOSQL system known as BigTable(columnbased or wide column stores), which is used in many of Google’s
applications that require vast amounts of data storage, such as
Gmail, Google Maps, and Website indexing.
❖ Apache HBase is an open-source NoSQL system based on similar
concepts.
❖ Amazon developed a NOSQL system called DynamoDB ( keyvalue data stores or key-object data stores) that is available
through Amazon’s cloud services.
❖ Facebook developed a NOSQL system called Cassandra, which is
now open source and known as Apache Cassandra. This NOSQL
system uses concepts from both key-value stores and columnbased systems.

5

Database Management Systems
Emergence of NoSQL systems
❖ Other software companies started developing
own solutions and making them available to
users who need these capabilities—for
example, MongoDB and CouchDB, which are
classified as document-based NOSQL systems
or document stores.

❖ Another category of NOSQL systems is the
graph-based NOSQL systems, or graph
databases; these include Neo4J and GraphBase.
6

Database Management Systems
Characteristics of NoSQL Systems

The characteristics of NoSQL systems can be divided into two
categories—
• those related to distributed databases and distributed systems,
• those related to data models and query languages.

7

Database Management Systems
Characteristics of NoSQL Systems
NOSQL Characteristics related to distributed databases and distributed systems
1. Scalability: In NOSQL systems, horizontal scalability is employed while the system is

operational, so techniques for distributing the existing data among new nodes without
interrupting system operation are necessary.
2. Availability, Replication and Eventual Consistency: Many applications that use NOSQL

systems require continuous system availability. To accomplish this, data is replicated
over two or more nodes transparently, so that if one node fails, the data is still available

on other nodes. Replication improves data availability and can also improve read
performance, because read requests can often be serviced from any of the replicated
data nodes.

Database Management Systems
Characteristics of NoSQL Systems

3. Replication Models: Two major replication models are used in NOSQL systems: master-slave

and master-master replication. Master-slave replication requires one copy to be the master
copy; all write operations must be applied to the master copy and then propagated to the slave
copies, usually using eventual consistency (the slave copies will eventually be the same as the

master copy).The master-master replication allows reads and writes at any of the replicas but
may not guarantee that reads at nodes that store different copies see the same values.
4. Sharding : Sharding of the file records is often employed in NOSQL systems. This serves to

distribute the load of accessing the file records to multiple nodes. The combination of sharding
the file records and replicating the shards works in tandem to improve load balancing as well as
data availability.

Database Management Systems
Characteristics of NoSQL Systems
5. High-Performance Data Access: In many NOSQL applications, it is necessary to find

individual records or objects (data items) from among the millions of data records or objects in a file. To achieve this, most systems use
one of two techniques: hashing or range partitioning on object keys.
• In hashing, a hash function h(K) is applied to the key K, and the location of the object

with key K is determined by the value of h(K).
• In range partitioning, the location is determined via a range of key values;

for example, location i would hold the objects whose key
values K are in the range Kiٖ min ≤ K ≤ Ki max.

Database Management Systems
Characteristics of NoSQL Systems
NOSQL characteristics related to data models and query languages.
1. Not Requiring a Schema: The flexibility of not requiring a schema is achieved in many NOSQL

systems by allowing semi-structured, self-describing data.
2. Less Powerful Query Languages: Many applications that use NOSQL systems may not require a

powerful query language such as SQL, because search (read) queries in these systems often
locate single objects in a single file based on their object keys. NOSQL systems typically provide
a set of functions and operations as a programming API, so reading and writing the data objects

is accomplished by calling the appropriate operations by the programmer.
3. Versioning: Some NOSQL systems provide storage of multiple versions of the data items, with

the timestamps of when the data version was created.

Database Management Systems
Categories of NoSQL systems
1. Document-based NOSQL systems: These systems store data in the form of documents using well-known formats,
such as JSON (JavaScript Object Notation).
Examples - CouchDB, MongoDB
2. NOSQL key-value stores: These systems have a simple data model based on fast access by the key to the value
associated with the key; the value can be a record or an object or a document or even have a more complex data

structure.
Examples - DynamoDB, Redis
3. Column-based / wide column NOSQL systems: These systems partition a table by column into column families (a
form of vertical partitioning), where each column family is stored in its own files.
Examples - Cassandra, HBase
4. Graph-based NOSQL systems: Data is represented as graphs, and related nodes can be found by traversing the edges
using path expressions. Example: neo4j

Database Management Systems
Categories of NoSQL systems

Database Management Systems
Categories of NoSQL systems

Database Management Systems
CAP Theorem

• The CAP theorem, which was originally introduced as the CAP principle, can be used to

explain some of the competing requirements in a distributed system with replication.

• The three letters in CAP refer to three desirable properties of distributed systems with

replicated data: consistency (among replicated copies), availability (of the system for read
and write operations) and partition tolerance (in the face of the nodes in the system being
partitioned by a network fault).

Database Management Systems
CAP Theorem

• Consistency means that the nodes will have the same copies of a replicated data

item visible for various transactions.
• Availability means that each read or write request for a data item will either be

processed successfully or will receive a message that the operation cannot be
completed.
• Partition tolerance means that the system can continue operating if the network

connecting the nodes has a fault that results in two or more partitions, where the
nodes in each partition can only communicate among each other.

Database Management Systems
CAP Theorem

The CAP theorem states that:
“It is not possible to guarantee all three of the desirable properties—
consistency, availability, and partition tolerance—at the same time in a
distributed system with data replication”.

Database Management Systems
CAP Theorem

Database Management Systems
CAP Theorem
• If this is the case, then the distributed system designer would have to choose two
properties out of the three to guarantee.
• It is generally assumed that in many traditional (SQL) applications, guaranteeing
consistency through the ACID properties is important. On the other hand, in a NOSQL
distributed data store, a weaker consistency level is often acceptable, and guaranteeing
the other two properties (availability, partition tolerance) is important.
• Hence, weaker consistency levels are often used in NOSQL system instead of
guaranteeing serializability. In particular, a form of consistency known as eventual
consistency is often adopted in NOSQL systems.

Database Management Systems
NOSQL Key-Value Stores

Introduction
● Focus on high performance, availability and scalability by storing the data in
“distributed storage systems”.

● Simple data model and in most cases no query language just a set of operations that
can be used by the application programmers.

● Key: A unique identifier associated with a data item and is used to locate this data
item rapidly.

● Value: The data item itself, and it can have very different formats for different keyvalue storage systems.

● In some cases the value is a string/array of bytes and application using the datastore
interprets the structure, in other cases a standard data format is allowed (ex. JSON)

Database Management Systems
Some examples of key-value databases
• Amazon DynamoDB: Part of Amazon’s AWS/SDK

Platforms and used as part of the Amazon cloud
computing services. The basic data model in DynamoDB
uses the concepts of tables, items, and attributes

• Redis: Redis is an open-source, in-memory data
structure store that is often used as a database, cache,
or message broker.
• Riak: It is the database used to develop applications.

Database Management Systems
Some examples of key-value databases
• Aerospike: It is an open-source and real-time database
working with billions of transactions.
• Berkeley DB: It is a high-performance and open-source
database providing scalability.
• Couchbase: It permits SQL-style querying and searching
for text.

Database Management Systems
Redis DB

Key Features and Characteristics:
• In-memory storage: Redis stores data in RAM, which allows for
extremely fast read and write operations compared to traditional diskbased databases.
• Key-value store: Redis uses a simple key-value data model, where each
piece of data is associated with a unique key.
• Data structures: Beyond basic strings, Redis supports various data
structures like lists, sets, sorted sets, hashes, and more, offering flexibility
in how you store and manipulate data.
• Optional persistence: While primarily in-memory, Redis offers options to
persist data to disk, either through snapshots or append-only files,
ensuring data durability.

Database Management Systems
Redis DB

Key Features and Characteristics:
• Replication: Redis supports master-slave replication for high
availability and read scaling.
• Clustering: Redis can be configured in a cluster for even greater
scalability and fault tolerance, distributing data across multiple
nodes.
• Lua scripting: Redis allows you to execute Lua scripts for complex
operations on the server side.
• Pub/Sub: Redis provides a publish/subscribe messaging system for
real-time communication between applications.

Database Management Systems
Redis DB

Use Cases:
• Caching: Redis is widely used as a caching layer to store frequently
accessed data closer to the application, reducing the load on primary
databases and improving response times.
• Session management: Storing user session data in Redis can improve
performance and scalability for web applications.
• Real-time analytics: Redis's speed and ability to handle large datasets
make it suitable for processing and analyzing real-time data streams.

Database Management Systems
Redis DB

Use Cases:
• Leaderboards and counters: Redis's data structures like sorted sets and
atomic operations make it ideal for implementing leaderboards,
counters, and other real-time rankings.
• Message queuing: Redis can act as a message broker for asynchronous
communication between different parts of an application.
• Vector database: Redis can be used as a vector database to store and
query vector embeddings, which are used in machine learning
applications.

Database Management Systems
Redis DB

Advantages:
• High performance: In-memory storage and optimized data
structures lead to low latency and high throughput.
• Flexibility: Redis supports various data structures and can be used
for diverse applications.
• Scalability: Redis can be scaled horizontally by distributing data
across multiple nodes.
• Open source: Being an open-source project, Redis has a large
community and extensive documentation.

Database Management Systems
Redis DB

Disadvantages:
• Limited data persistence: While Redis offers persistence options, it's
not designed as a primary, disk-based database. Data loss can occur
in the case of unexpected power outages or other hardware
failures.
• Not suitable for complex queries: Redis is optimized for key-value
lookups and simple operations. It's not ideal for complex joins,
aggregations, or other advanced database queries.
• Memory limitations: Because Redis stores data in memory, you
need to consider the amount of available RAM for your application.

Database Management Systems
Redis DB
Redis Data Structures
Redis provides a rich set of data structures that make it highly versatile and
powerful for different kinds of applications beyond simple key-value storage.
1. Strings
• Basic unit of data in Redis (text, numbers, binary data)
• Max size: 512 MB
• Example use: Caching, counters, session storage
2. Lists
• Ordered collection of strings (Linked List)
• Supports push/pop from both ends
• Example use: Message queues, task lists

Database Management Systems
Redis DB
Redis Data Structures
3. Sets
• Unordered collection of unique strings
• Example use: Tags, unique elements, social features (followers)
4. Sorted Sets (ZSets)
• Like Sets but each member has a score for ordering
• Example use: Leaderboards, ranking systems
5. Hashes
• Key-value pairs within a key (like a small dictionary)
• Example use: Store object-like data (user profiles, product details)
6. Bitmaps
• Bit-level operations on strings
• Example use: User activity tracking, presence indicators

Database Management Systems
NOSQL Key-Value Stores

● These are the simplest type of NoSQL databases. Data is stored as a key-value pair
● Key: represents a unique identifier (Like SRN) which is used to locate an item using
this key

● Value: represents the data (Like Name) itself which could be of various data formats
● Works like a giant dictionary / hash map that is persisted on disk
● These are usually schema-less i.e. , there are no fixed columns or table structure.
● Have high performance, scalability and flexibility.
● Common use cases include caching, implementing message queues using pub sub
architecture, fetching preferences or using session storage.

● Examples: Redis, Amazon DynamoDB, CouchDB, Aerospike

Database Management Systems
Redis DB

Introduction and Features:
● Redis is an open source, advanced key-value store
● Used for building high performance, scalable web applications.
● In memory: Redis holds its database entirely in the memory (RAM), using the disk only for
persistence.

● Versatile Data Structures: Redis has a relatively rich set of data types like hashes, lists, sets,
strings etc.

● Lua scripting support: Redis has built-in support for Lua scripting, which allows
developers to run custom code directly within the Redis server

● Replication and Clustering: Redis can scale horizontally with Redis Cluster (Clustering)
and also can replicate data to any number of slaves (Uses a master slave architecture)

Database Management Systems
Redis DB

Use Cases:
● Caching: We can cache frequently requested information to reduce the load on the main
database and boost an app’s performance. Ex: Zomato’s popular restaurants

● Message broker: Redis can act as a middleman, helping applications exchange data through
the asynchronous pub/sub architecture. Ex: Roblox notification system

● Streaming data processing and real-time analytics: Redis can be used for monitoring
website traffic, tracking user activity, analyzing financial transactions. Ex: Xignite

● Session storage: Things like login status and shopping cart info can be stored for a user to
prevent re-login on refreshing the page. Ex: Amazon shopping cart

● Game leaderboard: Redis can store the player’s profile and his score which can help in
calculating the leaderboard in real time. Ex: HackerRank Leaderboard system

Database Management Systems
Redis DB

Limitations:
● RAM is expensive: Redis for large datasets can become expensive, especially when scaling
up since RAM is significantly more expensive than disk storage.

● Manual memory management: Redis does not automatically manage memory like
relational databases do. Developers must manually configure eviction policies to decide what
happens when memory is full

● Potential data loss: Redis keeps everything in RAM, meaning that if the server unexpectedly
crashes, all the data may be lost.

● Not suitable for complex queries: Redis is optimized for key-value lookups and simple
operations. It's not ideal for complex joins, aggregations, or other advanced database queries.

Database Management Systems
Redis DB

Basics of Redis:
• Redis uses key value pairs. Each key is a string type (It’s binary safe so it can hold bytes not
•
•
•
•
•
•

just text)
Each value can be of different data structures (strings, hashes, sets explained further ahead)
Each key should be unique in Redis and : (colon) is used to show hierarchy (Similar to
namespaces concept you guys have learnt in OS)
If we want to store all information of PES in one place then we can create a key called PES
only
If we want to store all departments under PES university then we can create a key called
PES:Department where PES:Department is a single key
Please note that by default all values in the key-value pair of redis is strings
Check the next slide for better explanation and example

•

HSET is a command to store values in Hashes (explained later) and here key is user:user_id (user:101, user:102)

•

We need to have unique keys in Redis and to show it belongs to user table, hence we use this format

•

For every user id we can store the name and age fields in Redis just like MySQL (Note the colour coding for the
field and value)

Command

Description

Illustrative Query Example

EXPIRE key seconds

Sets a timeout on a key in seconds. The
key is deleted after the timeout.

EXPIRE user:session:123 3600

TTL key

Returns the remaining time to live of a key
in seconds.

PERSIST key

Removes the timeout on a key, making it
persistent.

Deletes one or more specified keys.

Database Management Systems
Redis DB
Data Structures in Redis:
Data structures are essential to store different formats of data in your keys.
We cover 5 of the most popular data structures in Redis in this course but feel free to explore more!

1. Strings:
▪ It is a binary sequence of bytes (characters). Max size is 512MB
▪ Use Case: CRUD Operations, Caching, Atomic Counters
2. Lists:
▪ Lists are sequences of strings that are built upon Linked Lists.
▪ Use Case: Task Lists (Stacks and queues), Logging
3. Hashes:
▪ Similar to your dictionaries in Python or JSON format. Map a key-value pair
▪ Use Case: Store structured data and mimic JSON format

Database Management Systems
Redis DB
Data Structures in Redis:
4.

Sets:

▪ Collection of unique strings where order is not preserved. (Similar to Sets in python)
▪ Use Case: Tracking unique items, Tagging
5.

Sorted Sets (Zsets):

▪ Data structure that combines the properties of a Set and a Scored collection (Similar to
priority queues)

▪ Every member stored in the Zset has a score associated and members are sorted based on
score (in case of a tie of score lexicographic sorting of members is done)

▪ Use Case: Leaderboard calculation, Ranking mechanism

SADD article:123 "redis" "database"

SREM key member [member...]

Removes one or more members from a set.

SREM article:123 "old_tag"

SMEMBERS key

Returns all the members of the set.

SMEMBERS article:123

SCARD key

Gets the number of members in a set.

SCARD article:123

SISMEMBER key member

Determines if a given value is a member of a
set.

SISMEMBER article:123 "redis“
(1 if present else 0)

SINTER key [key...]

Returns the intersection of multiple sets.

SINTER friends:alice friends:bob

SUNION key [key...]

Returns the union of multiple sets.

SUNION tech_tags devops_tags

SDIFF key [key...]

Returns the difference between the first set
and subsequent sets.

SDIFF group:a group:b

Sorted Sets (Zsets) Commands in Redis:
Command

Description

Example

ZADD key score member [score member...]

Adds or updates one or more members in a
sorted set with their scores.

ZADD leaderboard 1550 "player1"

ZINCRBY key increment member

Increments the score of a member by a
given amount.

ZINCRBY leaderboard 50 "player1"

ZRANGE key start stop

Returns a range of members by rank,
ordered by score (ascending).

ZRANGE leaderboard 0 9 WITHSCORES
(WITHSCORES gives the scores as well)

ZREVRANGE key start stop

Returns a range of members by rank,
ordered by score (descending).

ZREVRANGE leaderboard 0 9
(All ranges include both start and stop)

ZRANK key member

Returns the rank of a member (ascending
order).

ZRANK leaderboard "player1"

ZREM key member [member...]

Removes one or more members from a
sorted set.

ZREM leaderboard "player1"

ZCARD key

Gets the number of members in a sorted set. ZCARD leaderboard

Case Study: User Profile & Session Management

• You are building a new feature for a rapidly growing social media application.
• To ensure a fast and responsive user experience, you've decided to use Redis to cache
user profile information and manage temporary user data.

• Your user data model is simple. Each user has a unique ID (e.g., user:1, user:2)
[Hint: These are the names of your keys]

• You need to store their name and occasionally other attributes like height.

Database Management Systems
Redis DB
Based on the case study, write commands based on these scenarios:
1.

At the start of your application deployment, you need to set two user names at once (Vedant
and Bob was used for demo you can use anything).

2.

Fetch multiple users at once (the two users you created right now).

3.

A new user, Alice, has just signed up. You want to store her name and set a 60-second
expiration on the key since she is just a temporary user.

4.

User Bob (user:2) has decided to add his last name, "Ellison," to his profile. How can you
append "Ellison" to his existing name "Bob" to form "BobEllison"?

5.

User:1 wants to add his height as “120” com.

6.

How would you retrieve the height for user:1 ?

7.

During the poll, user:1 realizes they made a mistake and their height is actually 10cm taller.
Increment their stored height by 10 (without deleting and recreating the user)

2.

MGET user:1:name user:2:name => To fetch multiple user names

3.

SET user:3:name "Alice" EX 60 => Create user that lasts only for 60 seconds

4.

5.

SET user:1:height "120" => Create user 1’s height

6.

GET user:1:height => Fetch user 1’s height

7.

List Commands Demo in Redis:
RPUSH todolist "Do laundry" => To add event from right
2. LRANGE todolist 0 -1 => To fetch the todo list from 0 index (start index) to -1 index (last
index) [both inclusive] [Works like python where -1,-2,-3…]
3. LPUSH todolist "Call Dad" => To add event from left
4. LLEN todolist => To fetch length of list
5. LPOP todolist => To delete first element from left
6. RPOP todolist => To delete first element from right
7. RPUSH todolist "Buy groceries" => To add event from right
8. LTRIM todolist 0 0 => To trim the list to contain only elements within the range (Only 1st
task i.e. 0th index)

Sorted Set (Zset) Commands Demo in Redis:
ZADD leaderboard 100 "player1" 200 "player2" 300 "player3" => To add multiple player
names along with their scores (scores first then name)
2. ZCARD leaderboard => To fetch number of players in the set
3. ZREVRANGE leaderboard 0 -1 => To fetch player names in desc order within range (start
and stop index both inclusive)
4. ZREVRANGE leaderboard 0 -1 WITHSCORES => To fetch player names with scores in
desc order within range (start and stop index both inclusive)
5. ZINCRBY leaderboard 500 "player1" => To increase player 1’s score by 500
6. ZRANK leaderboard "player1" => To fetch rank of player 1 (Rank is done from lowest to
highest so last index has highest score)
7. ZREVRANK leaderboard "player1" => To fetch rank of player 1 (Rank is done from
highest to lowest so first index has highest score)
8. ZREM leaderboard "player1" => To remove player 1 from the set

Graph Databases(Neo4j)

Prof. Shilpa S

Database Management Systems
NOSQL Graph Databases and Neo4j
What is Neo4j?

•
•
•
•
•
•

Another category of NoSQL systems is known as graph databases (or graph-oriented
NoSQL systems).
Data is represented as a graph → a collection of nodes (vertices) and edges.
Nodes represent entities (e.g., Person, Product).
Edges represent relationships between nodes (e.g., FRIEND_OF, PURCHASED).
Many systems fall under graph databases, but our focus is on Neo4j.
Neo4j is an open-source graph database, implemented in Java.

Database Management Systems
NOSQL Graph Databases and Neo4j
Why is Neo4j Used?

•
•
•
•
•
•
•

Traditional relational DBs struggle with complex relationships (too many JOINs).
Neo4j is optimized for connected data and relationship-heavy queries.
Supports fast traversals by following direct links instead of rebuilding joins.
Allows intuitive modeling of real-world networks as graphs.
Flexible schema → can easily add new nodes, edges, or properties.
Supports Cypher Query Language (CQL) for simple, readable pattern matching.
Built-in graph algorithms → shortest path, PageRank, community detection.

Database Management Systems
NOSQL Graph Databases and Neo4j
Real-Life Applications of Neo4j?

•
•
•
•
•

Social Networks (Facebook, LinkedIn): friend suggestions, activity feeds.
E-commerce/Streaming (Amazon, Netflix): recommendation systems.
Banking & Finance (Mastercard): fraud detection by analyzing patterns.
Knowledge Graphs (Google, NASA): link and search across vast datasets.
IT & Network Management: track dependencies across systems and apps.

Database Management Systems
Neo4j Data Model
● Neo4j organizes data using the concepts of nodes and relationships.
● Both nodes and relationships can have properties, which store the data items associated with
nodes and relationships.
● Nodes can have labels (≥0), and the ones that have the same label are grouped into a collection
that identifies a subset of the nodes in the database graph for querying purposes.
● Relationships are directed; each relationship has a start node and end node as well as a
relationship type, which serves a similar role to a node label by identifying similar relationships
that have the same relationship type.
● Properties can be specified via a map pattern, which is made of one or more “name: value” pairs
for example {Lname: ‘Smith’, Fname: ‘John’, Minit: ‘B’}.
● To create nodes and relationships in Neo4j, we use the Neo4j CREATE command, which is part of
the high-level declarative query language Cypher.

Database Management Systems
Neo4j Data Model
●
●
●
●

Nodes in Neo4j correspond to entities
Node labels correspond to entity types and subclasses
Relationships correspond to relationship instances
Properties correspond to attributes
Neo4j

ER Model

Relationship is directed.

Relationship is not directed.

Node may have no label in Neo4j.

Every entity must belong to an entity type, so a
node must have a label.

Graph model of Neo4j is used as a basis for an
actual high-performance distributed database
system

The ER/EER(Enhanced ER) model is mainly used
for database design.

Database Management Systems
Node

Database Management Systems
Properties
•Both nodes and relationships can have properties.
•Properties are key-value pairs where the key is a
string.
•Property values can be either a primitive or an array
of one primitive type.
For example, String, int and int[] values are valid for
properties

Database Management Systems
Labels and Properties
● When a node is created, the node label can be specified.
It is also possible to create nodes without any labels.
● In Figure 24.4(a), the node labels are EMPLOYEE,
DEPARTMENT, PROJECT, and LOCATION; the created
nodes correspond to some of the data from the
COMPANY database.
● It is possible that some nodes have multiple labels.
● For example, the same node can be labeled as PERSON
and EMPLOYEE and MANAGER by listing all the labels
separated by the colon symbol as follows:
PERSON:EMPLOYEE: MANAGER.
● Having multiple labels is similar to an entity belonging to
an entity type (PERSON) plus some subclasses of PERSON
(namely EMPLOYEE and MANAGER).

Database Management Systems
Relationships and Relationship types

● Figure 24.4(b) shows a few example relationships in
Neo4j based on the COMPANY database
● The → specifies the direction of the relationship,
but the relationship can be traversed in either
direction.
● The relationship types (labels) in Figure 24.4(b) are
WorksFor, Manager, LocatedIn, and WorksOn
● Only relationships with the relationship type
WorksOn have properties (Hours) in Figure 24.4(b).

Database Management Systems
Relationship

Database Management Systems
Relationships

Database Management Systems
Path

A path is one or more nodes with connecting relationships, typically retrieved as a query or traversal result

Database Management Systems
Path

● A path specifies a traversal of part of the graph.
● It is typically used as part of a query to specify a pattern, where the query will retrieve from the
graph data that matches the pattern.
● A path is typically specified by a start node, followed by one or more relationships, leading to one
or more end nodes that satisfy the pattern.
● It is somewhat similar to the concepts of path expressions in the context of query languages for
object databases (OQL) and XML (XPat and XQuery).

Database Management Systems
Optional Schema
● A schema is optional in Neo4j.
● Graphs can be created and used without a schema, but in Neo4j version 2.0, a few schemarelated functions were added.
● The main features related to schema creation involve creating indexes and constraints based
on the labels and properties.
● For example, it is possible to create the equivalent of a key constraint on a property of a
label, so all nodes in the collection of nodes associated with the label must have unique
values for that property.

Database Management Systems
Indexing and node identifiers
● When a node is created, the Neo4j system creates an internal unique systemdefined identifier for each node.
● To retrieve individual nodes using other properties of the nodes efficiently, the
user can create indexes for the collection of nodes that have a particular label.
● Typically, one or more of the properties of the nodes in that collection can be
indexed.
● For example, Empid can be used to index nodes with the EMPLOYEE label, Dno to
index the nodes with the DEPARTMENT label, and Pno to index the nodes with the
PROJECT label.

Relationships
● Relationships are another major building block of a Graph Database. It
connects two nodes as depicted in the following figure.
● Here, Emp and Dept are two different nodes.
"WORKS_FOR" is a relationship between Emp
and Dept nodes.
● As it denotes, the arrow mark from Emp to Dept,
this relationship describes −

Database Management Systems
Neo4j - Practice Session

Relationships
● Each relationship contains one start node and one end node.
● Here, "Emp" is a start node, and "Dept" is an end node.
● As this relationship arrow mark represents a relationship from "Emp" node
to "Dept" node, this relationship is known as an "Incoming Relationship" to
"Dept" Node and "Outgoing Relationship" to "Emp" node.
● Like nodes, relationships also can contain properties as key-value pairs.
● Here, "WORKS_FOR" relationship has one property
as key-value pair.

● It represents an Id of this relationship.

Database Management Systems
Neo4j - Practice Session

Labels
● Label associates a common name to a set of nodes or relationships.
● A node or relationship can contain one or more labels.
● We can create new labels to existing nodes or relationships.
● We can remove the existing labels from the existing nodes or relationships.

For example-

● Left side node has a Label: "Emp" and the right side node has a Label:

"Dept".
● Relationship between those two nodes also has a Label: "WORKS_FOR".

Neo4j - Syntax
Prof. Shilpa S
Department of Computer Science and Engineering

Database Management Systems
Neo4j - Practice Session

Create a node:

Create (n)
// creates a single node
// n is an identifier and not the name of the node.
Output: Created one node
View a node:
Match(n) return(n);
//Fetches a node created in the database
//node is printed with internal property: “id”
// id is used for internal usage, starts from zero, and each node will have only one
internal id and unique in the database and is auto-generated by database

Database Management Systems
Neo4j - Practice Session

Create multiple nodes:
Create (n) (m)
// creates two nodes successfully. n and m are identifiers/variables
Output: Created two nodes
View all nodes created:
Match(n) return n;
//Fetches the nodes created in the database
Output: 3 nodes are displayed including the one created in previous slide

Match(n) return n limit 2
//Restrict the number of nodes by using limit clause Output: Displays first 2 nodes

Search a node by internal id:
match (n) where id(n) =1 return n
// id builtin function of Neo4j, returns the internal id of the node when used with where
clause
Output: Displays the node with internal id=1

Comparison operator to filter out the nodes (<,>,<=,>=,<>):
To fetch the node with internal id <=6
Match(n) where id(n)<=6 return n
Output: Displays all 7 nodes (node with id =0,1,2,3,4,5,6)
Search multiple nodes with internal id’s (using in clause)
Match(n) where id(n) in [1,2,0] return n
Output: Displays 3 nodes with id 0, 1, 2

Database Management Systems

Delete a node by its internal id:
match (n) where id(n) =1 delete n
// deletes the node that matches the where clause, ie deletes a node with id=1
Output: Deleted one node
Delete multiple nodes with id:
Match(n) where id(n) in [2,3] delete n
// two nodes are deleted with internal id 2 and 3
Output: Deleted two nodes
Delete all nodes
Match(n) delete n
//delete all the nodes in the database if there are no relationships in the graph
Output: Deleted “x” nodes

Create a node with properties (Properties are attributes of a node)

Properties are always associated with a node or relationship
Properties are optional attributes which are represented as key-value pairs as ({})
A node can have zero or more properties

create (x: Labelname {Property name: Value}) return x;

Tiger”

Pair of properties are separated by comma

x;

Key is the name of the property – String
Value can be primitive data type or can be array of primitive datatypes

Boolean, integer, double, long, character, string
Array of primitive datatypes:

Boolean array, int array, double array, long array, char array, string array

Creating a Complete Path
In Neo4j, a path is formed using continuous relationships. A path can be created
using the create clause.
CREATE p = (Node1 {properties})-[:Relationship_Type]-> (Node2
{properties})[:Relationship_Type]->(Node3 {properties}) RETURN p

Database Management Systems
Neo4j - Practice Session

Syntax for the MERGE command
MERGE (node: label {properties . . . . . . . })
Merging a Node with a Label
MERGE (node:label) RETURN node
MERGE (Jadeja:player) RETURN Jadeja
MERGE (CT2013:Tournament{name: "ICC Champions Trophy 2013"})
RETURN CT2013, labels(CT2013)

To delete multiple properties:
The match clause is used to search and remove a clause to delete certain property.
Method 1: Remove property by setting the value to NULL
Match(n:Book {author:“Neel”}) set n.publisher=NULL return n
//to remove - set publisher to null from the nodes with label Book and author “Neel”
//This removes property publisher.
Method 2: Remove property by setting the value to NULL
Match(n:Book) where n.author = “Neel” set n.publisher=NULL return n

Use Match clause to get all the nodes and remove clause to delete certain
property from all.

We can create a relationship using the CREATE clause. We will specify relationship within the

square braces “[ ]” depending on the direction of the relationship it is placed between
hyphen “ - ” and arrow “ → ” as shown in the following syntax.
Format : create (node1) – [:RelationshipType]->(node2)

Create two nodes and then relation ship between them
Create (chetan:author{name:”Chetan”,dob:1980,age:41})
Create (nagas:Book{title:”Nagas”,price:200,publication:”westland publication”}

//above to queries creates 2 nodes
Create (chetan)-[r:author_of]->(nagas)
//above query to create relationship between them

Introduction to Vector databases
Prof. Nivedita Kasturi
Department of Computer Science and Engineering

Database Management Systems
Introduction to Vector databases
•

Relational database stores data of type numbers, text, video url, image meta
data and BLOB data like images , video. CLOB data like large characters
like Pdf or word docs.

•

Data is stored as digital form in storage systems and DBMS facilitates the
Access, Modification and updates to databases.

•

DBMS often manages database as files. SQL databases traditionally support
primitive data types like integer, float, Boolean, and other data types. Many
data platforms are evolving to support more intricate data types such as
vectors to accommodate emerging needs like machine learning applications.

•

Vectors represent float array data from multimedia files, often provided as an
extension rather than a default data type

Database Management Systems
Introduction to Vector databases
• To understand why we are moving to vector databases or RDBMS with vector search options. We need
to know about full text search :
•

Full-text search (FTS) is a feature that allows users to efficiently search for words or phrases within
large textual columns (like articles, descriptions, documents) stored in a database. Unlike simple string
pattern matching (using LIKE or regular expressions), full-text search in a DBMS is designed to handle
large volumes of text data and to provide meaningful, relevance-ranked results.

• Vector search is better than traditional full-text search in DBMS because it retrieves results based on
semantic meaning, not just matching exact words or patterns. It uses high-dimensional embeddings
to find conceptually similar content, naturally handling synonyms, context, and user intent. This makes
it far more effective for modern applications like recommendation systems, question answering, and
multilingual search.

Database Management Systems
Introduction to Vector databases
Full-Text Search (FTS) in DBMS

Vector Databases (Semantic Search)

Finds documents by matching exact words or
stems.

Finds documents by matching meaning, using vector
similarity.

Works on tokens (lexemes): e.g., “run” =
“running”.

Works on embeddings (dense vectors) that capture
context & meaning.

Uses inverted index: maps words → document
IDs.

Uses vector index (like HNSW, IVF): clusters &
searches by distance.

Does not understand synonyms or context unless Naturally handles synonyms & context (e.g.,
explicitly added.
“doctor” ≈ “physician”).
Ranks by term frequency (TF-IDF, BM25).
Limited for unstructured data (images, audio,
multimodal).

Ranks by vector distance (cosine similarity,
Euclidean).
Works for text, images, audio — anything
convertible to vectors.

Database Management Systems
Introduction to Vector databases
• Definition:
Specialized system for handling high-dimensional vector data
Efficient at indexing, querying, and retrieving such data
Enables advanced analysis & similarity searches beyond traditional databases
• Example (Analogy):
Like a super-smart librarian who knows every book & how they’re connected by themes
Makes it easy to find books on similar topics, not just exact matches
• How It Works:
Organizes vast data points by similarity or context
Finds information based on semantic relevance rather than exact criteria
E.g., helps discover articles similar to a specific one
• Integration with AI:
Can be combined with LLMs to build Retrieval Augmented Generation (RAG) systems
Powers advanced search & content generation use cases

Many companies are
continually raising
money to develop
their
vector
databases or to add
vector
search
capabilities to their
existing SQL or
NoSQL databases.

Database Management Systems
Introduction to Vector databases
Many companies are continually raising money to develop their vector databases or to add vector
search capabilities to their existing SQL or NoSQL databases. Today, there are a number of different
vector databases available like chroma, weaviate, etc.

Database Management Systems
Vector Databases
• Vector databases have gained significant importance in various
fields due to their unique ability to efficiently store, index, and
search high-dimensional data points, often referred to as vectors.
• These databases are designed to handle data where each entry is
represented as a vector in a multi-dimensional space.
• The vectors can represent a wide range of information, such as
numerical features, embeddings from text or images, and even
complex data like molecular structures.
• Let’s represent the vector database using a 2D grid where one
axis represents the color of the animal (brown, black, white) and
the other axis represents the size (small, medium, large).

Database Management Systems
Vector Databases
Example:
• Like a big box of colorful crayons — each crayon is
different.
• A vector database acts like a magical sorting
machine.
• When you show it your favorite blue crayon,
it quickly finds other crayons closest in color.
• Saves time by finding similar items without
searching through the whole box!

Database Management Systems
How Do Vector Databases Store Data?
• Store data as high-dimensional vector embeddings
capturing semantic meaning & relationships
• Use specialized indexing (hashing, quantization,
graph-based) for fast similarity searches
• Ideal for unstructured data like text, images, audio
• Handle semantically similar retrieval tasks
efficiently
• Designed to scale with AI workloads, despite being
computationally intensive
• Critical for modern data management, managing
complex data relationships

Database Management Systems
How does a vector database differ from a traditional database?

Database Management Systems
Vector Database Capabilities
The significance of vector databases lies in their capabilities and applications:
- Efficient Similarity Search:
• Vector databases excel at performing similarity searches, where you can retrieve vectors that
are most similar to a given query vector.
- High-Dimensional Data:
• Vector databases are designed to handle high-dimensional data more efficiently, making them
suitable for applications like natural language processing, computer vision, and genomics.
- Machine Learning and AI:
• Vector databases are often used to store embeddings generated by machine learning models.
These embeddings capture the essential features of the data and can be used for various tasks,
such as clustering, classification, and anomaly detection.

Database Management Systems
Vector Database Capabilities
Real-time Applications:
Many vector databases are optimized for real-time or near-real-time querying, making them suitable for
applications that require quick responses, such as recommendation systems in e-commerce, fraud
detection, and monitoring IoT sensor data.
Personalization and User Profiling:
Vector databases enable personalized experiences by allowing systems to understand and predict user
preferences. This is crucial in platforms like streaming services, social media, and online marketplaces.
Spatial and Geographic Data:
Vector databases can handle geographic data, such as points, lines, and polygons, efficiently. This is
essential in applications like geographical information systems (GIS), location-based services, and
navigation applications.

Database Management Systems
Vector Database Capabilities

Healthcare and Life Sciences:
In genomics and molecular biology, vector databases are used to store and analyze genetic
sequences, protein structures, and other molecular data.
Data Fusion and Integration:
Vector databases can integrate data from various sources and types, enabling more comprehensive
analysis and insights. This is valuable in scenarios where data comes from multiple modalities, such
as combining text, image, and numerical data.
Multilingual Search:
Vector databases can be used to create powerful multilingual search engines by representing text
documents as vectors in a common space, enabling cross-lingual similarity searches.

Database Management Systems
Traditional Database's VS Vector Databases

Database Management Systems
Q&A

Which of the following best describes a primary advantage of vector databases over traditional
relational databases?
A) They store structured tabular data more efficiently
B) They allow faster transaction processing for numeric data
C) They enable similarity searches based on semantic meaning
D) They use B-tree indexes for optimizing join operations
Answer: C
Explanation: Vector databases are designed to perform efficient similarity searches based on highdimensional vector embeddings that capture semantic relationships, unlike traditional relational
databases which rely on structured tabular data and exact matches.

Database Management Systems
Q&A

In the context of database systems, what does full-text search (FTS) primarily enable?
A) Retrieving documents by matching semantic similarity of content
B) Searching large text columns by tokenizing and indexing words
C) Efficient storage of high-dimensional vectors from machine learning models
D) Generating vector embeddings for text and images
Answer: B
Explanation: Full-text search in traditional DBMS breaks text into tokens (words), builds an
index, and allows searching large text fields efficiently based on exact or normalized word
matches.

Database Management Systems
Q&A

Which of the following is a typical use case for vector databases?
A) Maintaining foreign key constraints across multiple tables
B) Performing multilingual similarity search across text documents
C) Running aggregate queries like SUM and COUNT on numeric fields
D) Implementing ACID transactions for bank account balances
Answer: B
Explanation: Vector databases excel at handling tasks like multilingual search, where text
from different languages is represented in a shared vector space for similarity retrieval, a
scenario beyond the scope of traditional relational operations.

Understanding Vectors
• A vector is a dynamic array of
numbers, and they are commonly
used in math and physics to
describe spatial coordinates.
• For
example,
the
twodimensional vector (2.45, 1.28)
expresses the location of a point in
two-dimensional space:

• Vectors in Math & Physics:
Physics: quantity with magnitude & direction
Math: geometric entity showing magnitude &
direction
• Simplified view:
A vector = list of attributes of an object
E.g., a house: [bedrooms, bathrooms, area, price]

Database Management Systems
Understanding Vectors
• In machine learning, vectors are still a
dynamic array of numbers, but each number
describes a feature of a particular data point.
• For example, if you had a dataset about
flowers that contained data about two different
features (petal length and petal width), each
flower could be represented as a twodimensional
vector:

• Plotting these vectors on a graph allows us to
see that the data points for flowers FL1 and
FL4 are the closest, which means they're the
most similar to each other.

Database Management Systems
Understanding Vector Databases
• A single vector always corresponds to
one particular object — for example, a
word, an image, a video, or a
document.
• Once an embedding model has
generated some vectors, they can be
stored in a vector database, alongside
the original content that they represent
— such as a page URL, link to an
image, or a product SKU(Stock
Keeping Unit).
• Vectors are sometimes called vector
embeddings — this just means a vector
that was produced by an embedding
model.

• Vector Database:
Stores vectors that describe objects or data points
Defines relationships between these vectors
• Examples:
Text: Vectors for words, paragraphs, documents (chatbots,
search)
Images: Pixels as numbers → combined into highdimensional vectors
Audio: Sound waves → numerical data → vectors (voice
recognition)

Database Management Systems
Understanding Vector Databases
• Example, in a scenario where an ML application is
tasked with recommending products based on
previous purchases, it would need to determine which
products are similar to each other.
• In the Given example, there are clusters for each of
clothing, phones, and board games, but these clusters
are located further apart as they are less similar to
each other.
• Within the clothing category itself, similar items are
closer to each other.
• For example, "black t-shirt" and "black polo shirt" are
extremely close to each other (being the same color
and shape), whereas "blue jeans" is further away.

Database Management Systems
Understanding Embedding
• Embedding models help us work out which objects are similar by converting very complex data
(such as text, images, documents or videos) into vectors, which consist of numerical data.
• Given that we need to quantify which objects are similar, it really helps to have numerical data for
solving this problem.
• Converting data into vectors is a way of simplifying data, while still retaining the most important
features of it.
• Words are represented as vectors in a high-dimensional space, where each dimension of the space
corresponds to a specific aspect or feature of the word's meaning.
• For example, dimensions might represent things like context, semantics, or syntax.
• The procedure to generate the vectors from words is called embeddings. ( Text, documents, sentences,
chat messages , Images , Audio, Videos. These data types are converted into vector embeddings )

Database Management Systems
Understanding Embedding
• Vector embeddings are numerical interpretations that retain the contextual significance of data ,
facilitating the alignment of similar entities within a vector space for similarity searches.
• Vector embeddings numerically capture the semantic meaning of the objects in relation to other
objects. Thus, similar objects are grouped together in the vector space, which means the closer two
objects are, the more similar they are.
• For example, closely related concepts like "Boats" and "Ferries" may be positioned near each other
due to their contextual links.

Figure 1. Text-to-Vector Transformation Using an
Embedding Mode

• There are several pre-trained embedding
models available, such as those from
OpenAI, Google, MetaAI, or the opensource community.
• These models learn from a lot of text to
map words into a multi-dimensional

vector space.
• The location of a data point in the vector
space tells us which words are related to
each other.

Database Management Systems
Understanding Embedding
• While the embeddings can be stored as numeric arrays, searching the arrays stored in SQL
databases can be memory-intensive and time-consuming.
• Every time a search operation needs to be done, all the vectors are retrieved, the cosine
similarity is calculated, and the matching string is returned based on the value of cosine
similarity or distance.
• The vector length for image, video, and audio data tends to be much bigger than the text data.
• To provide for this situation, most RDBMS systems now provide a wrapper or add-on on top
of the existing RDBMS that allows having a vector as a data type.
• And allows retrieving the data intrinsically based on the similarity between vectors rather
than manually calculating the distance or cosine similarity.

Database Management Systems
Understanding Embedding

• Pretrained machine learning models trained on millions of data items create these embeddings.
• Some of the most commonly used models model resources that aid in creating vector
embeddings for text-based data include Hugging Face that creates text embeddings using
Word2Vec, Glove, and Paragram.
• TensorFlow universal sentence encoder that creates embeddings using deep learning models
optimized for greater-than-word length text.
• OpenAI that creates text embeddings using transformers like GPT models. And
GoogleGenerativeAI that creates text embeddings using machine learning techniques such as
BERT and other transformer architectures.

Database Management Systems
Understanding Embedding
• Embedding models (vectorizers):Generate vectors from unstructured data
(words, sentences, docs)
• Examples:
• OpenAI: text-embedding-ada-002 (used by GPT models)
• Google: embedding-gecko-001 (used by PaLM 2)
• Each model has its own technique for creating embeddings

Steps for generating embeddings

Database Management Systems
Understanding Embedding
Steps for generating Embedding for products relational DB :
Step 1: Extract Data from Relational Table
Connect to your database (e.g., PostgreSQL)
Select the text field to be embedded (e.g., product description)
Step 2: Generate Embeddings
Use a sentence embedding model (sentence-transformers)
Convert each row's text into a high-dimensional vector (embedding)
Step 3: Store Embeddings into DB
Use pgvector to add a vector column
Update each record with the generated vector
Step 4: Query Using Similarity
Use vector similarity operators (like <=>)
Find semantically similar rows (nearest neighbors)
Note: Refer to code file “54_code for VectorSearch with RelationalDB”

Database Management Systems
How Vector Databases Work with Embeddings
• Data Processing:
• User query may involve raw data: images, documents, videos,
audio
• Data can be structured or unstructured
• Embedding Generation:
• Processed through an embedding model (complex neural network)
• Converts data into high-dimensional vectors
• Encodes data’s characteristics as vector embeddings
• Stored in a vector database
• Retrieval & Similarity Search:
• On query, vector DB performs similarity searches
• Finds vectors most similar to the query vector
• Delivers relevant results quickly, handling complex searches
• Benefit:
• Enables rapid, accurate management of vast & varied data types
• Ideal for applications needing high-speed search & retrieval

Database Management Systems
Similarity Score
• There are various metrics to measure the distance between vector or
data points.
• Two commonly used metrics are Cosine and Euclidean.
• In the cosine metric, we determine similarity by calculating the
cosine value of the angle (θ) between two vectors. When the angle
between two vectors is close to zero, the cosine value is close to 1.
Conversely, if the angle is 90 degrees (referred to as orthogonal
vectors in mathematics), the cosine value is zero.

Database Management Systems
Similarity Score
• We can use Cosine metric to calculate similarity between sentence.
For example,
• let’s consider a new sentence: “Rainy days make me sad”.
• If we want to find a sentence with a similar meaning from our existing list of sentences, calculating
the cosine for each sentence provides values like these.
‘Rainy days can be gloomy’ is the most
similar sentence to our new one. Both
convey negative feelings about rain. On
the other hand, ‘Success is rewarding’
yields the smallest cosine value, which
makes sense as it expresses a positive
sentiment.
🡺 That is precisely the objective with the
vector database, to rapidly identify
similar entries

Database Management Systems
Vector Search in Vector Databases
• Vector search compares similarity between vectors
• Each vector = an object or data point
• Retrieves objects similar to a query vector by analyzing semantic meaning
Distance Metrics:
• Used to quantify similarity between vectors
Common metrics:
• Euclidean distance
• Manhattan distance
• Cosine similarity
• Helps find nearest, most relevant results
Vector Indexing:
• Vector database used several Approximate Nearest Neighbor algorithms to speed up the similarity
search process. Organizes vectors to speed up retrieval, critical for large datasets
• Uses clustering & indexing algorithms (e.g., HNSW)
• Enables fast, scalable similarity searches

Database Management Systems
Vector Search in Vector Databases
• Popular Algorithms:
• KNN (K-Nearest Neighbors):
• Finds exact nearest neighbors based on distance
• Good for small to medium datasets
• ANN (Approximate Nearest Neighbors):
• Finds approximate nearest neighbors
• Improves efficiency for large, high-dimensional data
• HNSW (Hierarchical Navigable Small World):
• Builds hierarchical graph structure during indexing
• Enables fast neighbor queries, balancing accuracy & speed
• Used in vector DBs like Azure AI Search for ANN searches

Database Management Systems
Indexing in Vector Search
• Flat Index:
Stores vectors in a flat, unstructured format
No precomputed structure → each query scans all vectors
Used as a baseline to compare with more advanced indexes
• IVF (Inverted File):
Partitions dataset into clusters, each with an inverted list
During search, only relevant clusters are scanned
Greatly reduces computation & speeds up retrieval
Often combined with Product Quantization (PQ) for high-dimensional data
• VPT (Vantage-Point Tree):
Selects a vantage point & splits data based on distance threshold
Recursively builds a tree to exclude large portions during search
Efficient for low-dimensional spaces; less effective in high dimensions due to curse of dimensionality

Database Management Systems
Advanced Indexing Algorithms
• HNSW (Hierarchical Navigable Small World):
Builds a hierarchical graph to index high-dimensional vectors
Enables fast storage & search with minimal memory use
Ideal for scalable vector search
• IVFADC (Inverted File with Approximate Distance Calculation):
Uses inverted index structure for high-dimensional vectors
Known for fast search speed on large-scale datasets
• IVFPQ (Inverted File with Product Quantization):
• Applies product quantization to compress vectors before indexing
• Achieves high-accuracy search on massive datasets
• Efficient for memory and computation

Database Management Systems
Q&A

Which of the following is used to measure similarity
between vectors in vector search?
a) Primary key indexing
b) Cosine similarity
c) Sorting by timestamps
d) Hashing
Answer: b) Cosine similarity

Which indexing method combines inverted files
with vector compression to achieve highaccuracy searches on massive datasets?
a) Flat index
b) IVFPQ
c) Vantage-Point Tree
d) HNSW
Answer: b) IVFPQ

What is a primary benefit of using an HNSW index in vector search?
a) It guarantees exact nearest neighbors with exhaustive search
b) It compresses data using product quantization
c) It builds a hierarchical graph for fast & memory-efficient search
d) It only works for low-dimensional data
Answer: c) It builds a hierarchical graph for fast & memory-efficient search
